procedure

Theorem C.46
.

Given
q
matrices
U_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n_{1}}
,
U_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n_{2}}
,
\cdots
,
U_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n_{q}}
, let
\max_{i}n_{i}\leq n
. There exists an algorithm that takes
O_{q}(n\cdot\poly(k,1/\epsilon)\cdot R_{\mathrm{samples}})
time and samples
R_{\mathrm{samples}}
columns of
U_{1}\odot U_{2}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times\prod_{i=1}^{q}n_{i}}
according to the leverage scores of
U_{1}\odot U_{2}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q}
.

Proof.

Let
\max_{i}n_{i}\leq n
. First, choosing
\Pi_{0}
to be a
TensorSketch
, we can compute
R^{-1}
in
O_{q}(n\poly(k,1/\epsilon))
time, where
R
is the
R
in a QR-factorization. We want to sample columns from
U_{1}\odot U_{2}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q}
according to the square of the
\ell_{2}
-norm of each column of
R^{-1}(U_{1}\odot U_{2}\odot\cdots U_{q})
. The issue is the number of columns of this matrix is already
\prod_{i=1}^{q}n_{i}
. The goal is to sample columns from
R^{-1}(U_{1}\odot U_{2}\odot\cdots U_{q})
without explicitly computing the square of the
\ell_{2}
-norm of each column.

Similarly as in the proof of Lemma
C.32
, we have the observation that the following two sampling procedures are equivalent in terms of sampling a column of a matrix:
(1) We sample a single entry from matrix
R^{-1}(U_{1}\odot U_{2}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q})
proportional to its squared value, (2) We sample a column from matrix
R^{-1}(U_{1}\odot U_{2}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q})
proportional to its squared
\ell_{2}
-norm. Let the
(i,j_{1},j_{2},\cdots,j_{q})
-th entry denote the entry in the
i
-th row and the
j
-th column, where

\displaystyle j=\sum_{l=1}^{q-1}(j_{l}-1)\prod_{t=l+1}^{q}n_{t}+j_{q}.

Similarly to Equation (
C.7.4
), we can show, for a particular column
j
,

\displaystyle\Pr[\text{we\penalty\ sample\penalty\ an\penalty\ entry\penalty\ from\penalty\ the\penalty\ }j\text{-th\penalty\ column\penalty\ of\penalty\ matrix}]=\Pr[\text{we\penalty\ sample\penalty\ the\penalty\ }j\text{-th\penalty\ column\penalty\ of\penalty\ a\penalty\ matrix}].

Thus, it is sufficient to show how to sample a single entry from matrix
R^{-1}(U_{1}\odot U_{2}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q})
proportional to its squared value without writing down all the entries of the
k\times\prod_{i=1}^{q}n_{i}
matrix.

Let
V_{0}
denote
R^{-1}
. Let
n_{0}
denote the number of rows of
V_{0}
.

In the next few paragraphs, we describe a sampling procedure (procedure
FastTensorLeverageScoreGeneralOrder
in Algorithm
15
) which first samples
\widehat{j}_{0}
from
[n_{0}]
, then samples
\widehat{j}_{1}
from
[n_{1}]
,
\cdots
, and at the end samples
\widehat{j}_{q}
from
[n_{q}]
.

In the first step, we want to sample
\widehat{j}_{0}
from
[n_{0}]
proportional to the squared
\ell_{2}
-norm of that row. To do this efficiently, we choose
\Pi_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{\prod_{i=1}^{q}n_{i}\times s_{1}}
to be a
TensorSketch
to sketch on the right of
V_{0}(U_{1}\odot U_{2}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q})
. By Section
B.10
, as long as
s_{1}=O_{q}(\poly(k,1/\epsilon))
, then
\Pi_{1}
is a
(1\pm\epsilon)
-subspace embedding matrix. Thus with probability
1-1/\Omega(q)
, for all
i\in[n_{0}]
,

\displaystyle\|(V_{0})^{i}((U_{1}\odot U_{2}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q})\Pi_{1})\|_{2}^{2}=(1\pm\epsilon)\|(V_{0})^{i}((U_{1}\odot U_{2}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q}))\|_{2}^{2},

which means we can sample
\widehat{j}_{0}
from
[n_{0}]
in
O_{q}(n\poly(k,1/\epsilon))
time.

In the second step, we have already obtained
\widehat{j}_{0}
. Using that row of
V_{0}
with
U_{1}
, we can form a new matrix
V_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times k}
in the following sense,

\displaystyle(V_{1})^{i}=(V_{0})^{\widehat{j}_{0}}\circ(U_{1})_{i}^{\top},\forall i\in[n_{1}],

where
(V_{1})^{i}
denotes the
i
-th row of matrix
V_{1}
,
(V_{0})^{\widehat{j}_{0}}
denotes the
\widehat{j}_{0}
-th row of
V_{0}
and
(U_{1})_{i}
is the
i
-th column of
U_{1}
. Another important observation is, the entry in the
(j_{1},j_{2},\cdots,j_{q})
-th coordinate of vector
(V_{0})^{\widehat{j}_{0}}(U_{1}\odot U_{2}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q})
is the same as the entry in the
j_{1}
-th row and
(j_{2},\cdots,j_{q})
-th column of matrix
V_{1}(U_{2}\odot U_{3}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q})
. Thus, sampling
j_{1}
is equivalent to sampling
j_{1}
from the new matrix
V_{1}(U_{2}\odot U_{3}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q})
proportional to the squared
\ell_{2}
-norm of that row. We still have the computational issue that the length of the row vector is very long. To deal with this, we can choose
\Pi_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{\prod_{i=2}^{q}n_{i}\times s_{2}}
to be a
TensorSketch
to multiply on the right of
V_{1}(U_{2}\odot U_{3}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q})
.

By Section
B.10
, as long as
s_{2}=O_{q}(\poly(k,1/\epsilon))
, then
\Pi_{2}
is a
(1\pm\epsilon)
-subspace embedding matrix. Thus with probability
1-1/\Omega(q)
, for all
i\in[n_{1}]
,

\displaystyle\|(V_{1})^{i}((U_{2}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q})\Pi_{2})\|_{2}^{2}=(1\pm\epsilon)\|(V_{1})^{i}((U_{2}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q}))\|_{2}^{2},

which means we can sample
\widehat{j}_{1}
from
[n_{1}]
in
O_{q}(n\poly(k,1/\epsilon))
time.

We repeat the above procedure until we obtain each of
\widehat{j}_{0},\widehat{j}_{1},\cdots,\widehat{j}_{q}
. Note that the last one,
\widehat{j}_{q}
, is easier, since the length of the vector is already small enough, and so we do not need to use
TensorSketch
for it.

By Section
B.10
, the time for multiplying by
TensorSketch
is
O_{q}(n\poly(k,1/\epsilon))
. Setting
\epsilon
to be a small constant, and taking a union bound over
O(q)
events completes the proof.
∎

Lemma C.47
.

Given
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{0}\times\prod_{i=1}^{q}n_{i}}
,
U_{1},U_{2},\cdots,U_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n}
, for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists an algorithm that runs in
O(n\cdot\poly(k,1/\epsilon))
time and outputs a diagonal matrix
D\in\mathbb{R}^{\prod_{i=1}^{q}n_{i}\times\prod_{i=1}^{q}n_{i}}
with
m=O(k\log k+k/\epsilon)
nonzero entries such that,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{U}(U_{1}\odot U_{2}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q})-A\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}\|U(U_{1}\odot U_{2}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q})-A\|_{F}^{2},

holds with probability at least
0.999
, where
\widehat{U}
denotes the optimal solution of

\displaystyle\min_{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{0}\times k}}\|U(U_{1}\odot U_{2}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q})D-AD\|_{F}^{2}.

Proof.

This follows by combining Theorem
C.46
, Corollary
C.30
, and Lemma
C.31
.
∎

C.10.2
General iterative existential proof

Algorithm 16

General
q
-th Order Iterative Existential Proof

1:

procedure

GeneralIterativeExistentialProof
(
A,n,k,q,\epsilon
)
\triangleright
Section
C.10.2

2:

Fix
U_{1}^{*},U_{2}^{*},\cdots,U_{q}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
.

3:

for

i=1\to q

do

4:

Choose sketching matrix
S_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{q-1}\times s_{i}}
with
s_{i}=O_{q}(k/\epsilon)
.

5:

Define
Z_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n^{q-1}}
to be
\underset{j<i}{\odot}\widehat{U}_{j}^{\top}\odot\underset{j^{\prime}>i}{\odot}U^{*\top}_{j^{\prime}}
.

6:

Let
A_{i}
denote the matrix obtained by flattening tensor
A
along the
i
-th dimension.

7:

Define
\widehat{U}_{i}
to be
A_{i}S_{i}(Z_{i}S_{i})^{\dagger}
.

8:

end

for

9:

return

\widehat{U}_{1},\widehat{U}_{2},\cdots,\widehat{U}_{q}
.

10:

end

procedure

Given a
q
-th order tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times\cdots\times n}
, we fix
U_{1}^{*},U_{2}^{*},\cdots,U_{q}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
to be the best rank-
k
solution (if it does not exist, then we replace it by a good approximation, as discussed). We define
\OPT=\|U_{1}^{*}\otimes U_{2}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes U_{q}^{*}-A\|_{F}^{2}
. Our iterative proof works as follows. We first obtain the objective function,

\displaystyle\min_{U_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}\|U_{1}\cdot Z_{1}-A_{1}\|_{F}^{2}\leq\OPT,

where
A_{1}
is a matrix obtained by flattening tensor
A
along the first dimension,
Z_{1}=(U_{2}^{*\top}\odot U_{3}^{*\top}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q}^{*\top})
denotes a
k\times n^{q-1}
matrix. Choosing
S_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{q-1}\times s_{1}}
to be a Gaussian sketching matrix with
s_{1}=O(k/\epsilon)
, we obtain a smaller problem,

\displaystyle\min_{U_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}\|U_{1}\cdot Z_{1}S_{1}-A_{1}S_{1}\|_{F}^{2}.

We define
\widehat{U}_{1}
to be
A_{1}S_{1}(Z_{1}S_{1})^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, which gives,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{U}_{1}\cdot Z_{1}-A_{1}\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon)\OPT.

After retensorizing the above, we have,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{U}_{1}\otimes U_{2}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes U_{q}^{*}-A\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon)\OPT.

In the second round, we fix
\widehat{U}_{1}
,
U_{3}^{*}
,
\cdots
,
U_{q}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, and choose
S_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{q-1}\times s_{2}}
to be a Gaussian sketching matrix with
s_{2}=O(k/\epsilon)
. We define
Z_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n^{q-1}}
to be
(\widehat{U}_{1}^{\top}\odot U_{3}^{*\top}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q}^{*\top})
. We define
\widehat{U}_{2}
to be
A_{2}S_{2}(Z_{2}S_{2})^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{U}_{1}\otimes\widehat{U}_{2}\otimes U_{3}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes U_{q}^{*}-A\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon)^{2}\OPT.

We repeat the above process, where in the
i
-th round we fix
\widehat{U}_{1},\cdots,\widehat{U}_{i-1}
,
U_{i+1}^{*}
,
\cdots
,
U_{q}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, and choose
S_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{q-1}\times s_{i}}
to be a Gaussian sketching matrix with
s_{i}=O(k/\epsilon)
. We define
Z_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n^{q-1}}
to be
(\widehat{U}_{1}^{\top}\odot\cdots\odot\widehat{U}_{i-1}^{\top}\odot U_{i+1}^{*\top}\odot\cdots\odot U_{q}^{*\top})
. We define
\widehat{U}_{i}
to be
A_{i}S_{i}(Z_{i}S_{i})^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{U}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widehat{U}_{i-1}\otimes\widehat{U}_{i}\otimes U_{i+1}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes U_{q}^{*}-A\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon)^{2}\OPT.

At the end of the
q
-th round, we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{U}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widehat{U}_{q}-A\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon)^{q}\OPT.

Replacing
\epsilon=\epsilon^{\prime}/(2q)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\widehat{U}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widehat{U}_{q}-A\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon^{\prime})\OPT.

where for all
i\in[q]
,
s_{i}=O(kq/\epsilon^{\prime})=O_{q}(k/\epsilon^{\prime})
.

C.10.3
General input sparsity reduction

This section shows how to extend the input sparsity reduction from third order tensors to general
q
-th order tensors. Given a tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times\cdots\times n}
and
q
matrices, for each
i\in[q]
, matrix
V_{i}
has size
V_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times b_{i}}
, with
b_{i}\leq\poly(k,1/\epsilon)
. We choose a batch of sparse embedding matrices
T_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{t_{i}\times n}
. Define
\widehat{V}_{i}=T_{i}V_{i}
, and
C=A(T_{1},T_{2},\cdots,T_{q})
. Thus we have with probability
99/100
, for any
\alpha\geq 0
, for all
\{X_{i},X_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{i}\times k}\}_{i\in[q]}
,
if

\displaystyle\|\widehat{V}_{1}X_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\widehat{V}_{2}X_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widehat{V}_{q}X_{q}^{\prime}-C\|_{F}^{2}\leq\alpha\|\widehat{V}_{1}X_{1}\otimes\widehat{V}_{2}X_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widehat{V}_{q}X_{q}-C\|_{F}^{2},

then

\displaystyle\|V_{1}X_{1}^{\prime}\otimes V_{2}X_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{q}X_{q}^{\prime}-A\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon)\alpha\|V_{1}X_{1}\otimes V_{2}X_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{q}X_{q}-A\|_{F}^{2},

where
t_{i}=O_{q}(\poly(b_{i},1/\epsilon))
.

Algorithm 17

General
q
-th Order Input Sparsity Reduction

1:

procedure

GeneralInputSparsityReduction
(
A,\{V_{i}\}_{i\in[q]},n,k,q,\epsilon
)
\triangleright
Section
C.10.3

2:

for

i=1\to q

do

3:

Choose sketching matrix
T_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{t_{i}\times n}
with
t_{i}=\poly(k,q,1/\epsilon)
.

4:

\widehat{V}_{i}\leftarrow T_{i}V_{i}
.

5:

end

for

6:

C\leftarrow A(T_{1},T_{2},\cdots,T_{q})
.

7:

return

\{\widehat{V}_{i}\}_{i\in[q]},C
.

8:

end

procedure

C.10.4
Bicriteria algorithm

This section explains how to extend the bicriteria algorithm from third order tensors (Section
C.4
) to general
q
-th order tensors. Given any
q
-th order tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times\cdots\times n}
, we can output a
\rank
-
r
tensor (or equivalently
q
matrices
U_{1},U_{2},\cdots,U_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
) such that,

\displaystyle\|U_{1}\otimes U_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes U_{q}-A\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon)\OPT,

where
r=O_{q}((k/\epsilon)^{q-1})
and the algorithm takes
O_{q}(\nnz(A)+n\cdot\poly(k,1/\epsilon))
.

.

C.10.5

This section extends the tensor CURT algorithm from
3
rd order tensors (Section
C.7
) to general
q
-th order tensors. Given a
q
-th order tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times\cdots\times n}
and a batch of matrices
U_{1},U_{2},\cdots,U_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, we iteratively apply the proof in Theorem
C.40
(or Theorem
C.41
)
q
times. Then for each
i\in[q]
, we are able to select
d_{i}
columns from the
i
-th dimension of tensor
A
(let
C_{i}
denote those columns) and also find a tensor
U\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times\cdots\times d_{q}}
such that,

\displaystyle\|U(C_{1},C_{2},\cdots,C_{q})-A\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon)\|U_{1}\otimes U_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes U_{q}-A\|_{F}^{2},

where either
d_{i}=O_{q}(k\log k+k/\epsilon)
(similar to Theorem
C.40
) or
d_{i}=O_{q}(k/\epsilon)
(similar to Theorem
C.41
).

C.11
Matrix CUR decomposition

There is a long line of research on matrix CUR decomposition under operator, Frobenius or recently, entry-wise
\ell_{1}
norm
[
DMM08
,
BMD09
,
DR10
,
BDM11
,
BW14
,
SWZ17
]
. We provide the first algorithm that runs in
\nnz(A)
time, which improves the previous best matrix CUR decomposition algorithm under Frobenius norm
[
BW14
]
.

Theorem C.48
.

Given matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, for any
k\geq 1
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists an algorithm that takes
O(\nnz(A)+n\poly(k,1/\epsilon))
time and outputs three matrices
C\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times c}
with
c
columns from
A
,
R\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times n}
with
r
rows from
A
, and
U\in\mathbb{R}^{c\times r}
with
\rank(U)=k
such that
r=c=O(k/\epsilon)
and,

\displaystyle\|CUR-A\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{\rank-k\penalty\ A_{k}}\|A_{k}-A\|_{F}^{2},

holds with probability at least
9/10
.

Proof.

We define

\displaystyle\OPT=\min_{\rank-k\penalty\ A_{k}}\|A_{k}-A\|_{F}^{2}.

We first compute
\widehat{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
by using the result of
[
CW13
]
, so that
\widehat{U}
satisfies:

\displaystyle\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n}}\|\widehat{U}X-A\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon)\OPT.

(25)

This step can be done in
O(\nnz(A)+n\poly(k,1/\epsilon))
time.

We choose
S_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
to be a sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix according to the leverage scores of
\widehat{U}
, where here
s_{1}=O(\epsilon^{-2}k\log k)
is the number of samples. This step also can be done in
O(n\poly(k,1/\epsilon))
time.

We run
GeneralizedMatrixRowSubsetSelection
(Algorithm
7
) on matrices
S_{1}A
and
S_{1}\widehat{U}
. Then we obtain two new matrices
R
and
Y
, where
R
contains
r=O(k/\epsilon)
rows of
S_{1}A
and
Y
has size
k\times r
. According to Theorem
C.14
and Corollary
C.15
, this step takes
n\poly(k,1/\epsilon)
time.

We construct
\widehat{V}=YR
, and choose
S_{2}^{\top}
to be another sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix according to the leverage scores of
\widehat{V}^{\top}
with
s_{2}=O(\epsilon^{-2}k\log k)
nonzero entries. As in the case of constructing
S_{1}
, this step can be done in
O(n\poly(k,1/\epsilon))
time.

We run
GeneralizedMatrixRowSubsetSelection
(Algorithm
7
) on matrices
(AS_{2})^{\top}
and
(\widehat{V}S_{2})^{\top}
. Then we can obtain two new matrices
C^{\top}
and
Y^{\top}
, where
C^{\top}
contains
c=O(k/\epsilon)
rows of
(AS_{2})^{\top}
and
Z^{\top}
has size
k\times c
. According to Theorem
C.14
and Corollary
C.15
, this step takes
n\poly(k,1/\epsilon)
time.

Thus, overall the running time is
O(\nnz(A)+n\poly(k,1/\epsilon))
.

Correctness.

Let

\displaystyle X^{*}=\arg\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}\|X\widehat{V}-A\|_{F}^{2}.

According to Corollary
C.15
,

\displaystyle\|CZ\widehat{V}S_{2}-AS_{2}\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon^{\prime\prime})\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}\|X\widehat{V}S_{2}-AS_{2}\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon^{\prime\prime})\|X^{*}\widehat{V}S_{2}-AS_{2}\|_{F}^{2}.

According to Theorem
C.52
,
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}=0.001\epsilon^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\|CZ\widehat{V}-A\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon^{\prime})\|X^{*}\widehat{V}-A\|_{F}^{2}.

(26)

Let

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}=\arg\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n}}\|\widehat{U}X-A\|_{F}^{2}.

According to Corollary
C.15
,

\displaystyle\|S_{1}\widehat{U}YR-S_{1}A\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon^{\prime\prime})\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n}}\|S_{1}\widehat{U}X-S_{1}A\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon^{\prime\prime})\|S_{1}\widehat{U}\widetilde{X}-S_{1}A\|_{F}^{2}.

According to Theorem
C.52
, since
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}=0.001\epsilon^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{U}YR-A\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon^{\prime})\|\widehat{U}\widetilde{X}-A\|_{F}^{2}.

(27)

Then, we can conclude

\displaystyle\|CUR-A\|_{F}^{2}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|CZYR-A\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|CZ\widehat{V}-A\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1+\epsilon^{\prime})\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}\|X\widehat{V}-A\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1+\epsilon^{\prime})\|\widehat{U}\widehat{V}-A\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n}}\|\widehat{U}X-A\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{3}\OPT

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1+\epsilon)\OPT.

The first equality follows since
U=ZY
. The second equality follows since
YR=\widehat{V}
. The first inequality follows by Equation (
26
). The third inequality follows by Equation (
27
). The fourth inequality follows by Equation (
25
). The last inequality follows since
\epsilon^{\prime}=0.1\epsilon
.

Notice that
C
has
O(k/\epsilon)
reweighted columns of
AS_{2}
, and
AS_{2}
is a subset of reweighted columns of
A
, so
C
has
O(k/\epsilon)
reweighted columns of
A
. Similarly, we can prove that
R
has
O(k/\epsilon)
reweighted rows of
A
. Thus,
CUR
is a CUR decomposition of
A
.

∎

C.11.2
Stronger property achieved by leverage scores

Claim C.49
.

Given matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
, for any distribution
p=(p_{1},p_{2},\cdots,p_{n})
define random variable
X
such that
X=\|A_{i}\|_{2}^{2}/p_{i}
with probability
p_{i}
, where
A_{i}
is the
i
-th row of matrix
A
. Then take
m
independent samples
X^{1},X^{2},\cdots,X^{m}
, and let
Y=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}X^{j}
. We have

\displaystyle\Pr[Y\leq 100\|A\|_{F}^{2}]\geq.99.

Proof.

We can compute the expectation of
X^{j}
, for any
j\in[m]
,

\displaystyle\E[X^{j}]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\|A_{i}\|_{2}^{2}}{p_{i}}\cdot p_{i}=\|A\|_{F}^{2}.

Then
\E[Y]=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\E[X^{j}]=\|A\|_{F}^{2}
. Using Markov’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[Y\geq\|A\|_{F}^{2}]\leq.01.

∎

Theorem C.50

(The leverage score case of Theorem 39 in
[
CW13
]
)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
,
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. Let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
denote a sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix according to the leverage scores of
A
. If the event occurs that
S
satisfies
(\epsilon/\sqrt{k})
-Frobenius norm approximate matrix product for
A
, and also
S
is a
(1+\epsilon)
-subspace embedding for
A
, then let
X^{*}
be the optimal solution of
\min_{X}\|AX-B\|_{F}^{2},
and
\widetilde{B}\equiv AX^{*}-B
. Then, for all
X\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d},

\displaystyle(1-2\epsilon)\|AX-B\|_{F}^{2}\leq\|S(AX-B)\|_{F}^{2}+\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}-\|S\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+2\epsilon)\|AX-B\|_{F}^{2}.

Furthermore, if
S
has
m=O(\epsilon^{-2}k\log(k))
nonzero entries, the above event happens with probability at least
0.99
.

Note that Theorem 39 in
[
CW13
]
is stated in a way that holds for general sketching matrices. However, we are only interested in the case when
S
is a sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix according to the leverage scores. For completeness, we provide the full proof of the leverage score case with certain parameters.

Proof.

Suppose
S
is a sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix according to the leverage scores of
A
, and it has
m=O(\epsilon^{-2}k\log k)
nonzero entries. Then, according to Lemma
C.22
,
S
is a
(1+\epsilon)
-subspace embedding for
A
with probability at least
0.999
, and according to Lemma
C.29
,
S
satisfies
(\epsilon/\sqrt{k})
-Frobenius norm approximate matrix product for
A
with probability at least
0.999
.

Let
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
denote an orthonormal basis of the column span of
A
. Then the leverage scores of
U
are the same as the leverage scores of
A
. Furthermore, for any
X\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
, there is a matrix
Y
such that
AX=UY
, and vice versa, so we can now assume
A
has
k
orthonormal columns.

Then,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|S(AX-B)\|_{F}^{2}-\|S\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|SA(X-X^{*})+S(AX^{*}-B)\|_{F}^{2}-\|S\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|SA(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}+\|S(AX^{*}-B)\|_{F}^{2}+2\tr\left((X-X^{*})^{\top}A^{\top}S^{\top}S(AX^{*}-B)\right)-\|S\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \underbrace{\|SA(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}+2\tr\left((X-X^{*})^{\top}A^{\top}S^{\top}S\widetilde{B}\right)}_{\alpha}.

(28)

The second equality follows using
\|C+D\|_{F}^{2}=\|C\|_{F}^{2}+\|D\|_{F}^{2}+2\tr(C^{\top}D)
. The third equality follows from
\widetilde{B}=AX^{*}-B
.
Now, let us first upper bound the term
\alpha
in Equation (
28
):

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|SA(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}+2\tr\left((X-X^{*})^{\top}A^{\top}S^{\top}S\widetilde{B}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1+\epsilon)\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}+2\|X-X^{*}\|_{F}\|A^{\top}S^{\top}S\widetilde{B}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1+\epsilon)\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}+2(\epsilon/\sqrt{k})\cdot\|X-X^{*}\|_{F}\|A\|_{F}\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1+\epsilon)\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}+2\epsilon\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}.

The first inequality follows since
S
is a
(1+\epsilon)
subspace embedding of
A
, and
\tr(C^{\top}D)\leq\|C\|_{F}\|D\|_{F}
. The second inequality follows since
S
satisfies
(\epsilon/\sqrt{k})
-Frobenius norm approximate matrix product for
A
. The last inequality follows using that
\|A\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{k}
since
A
only has
k
orthonormal columns.
Now, let us lower bound the term
\alpha
in Equation (
28
):

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|SA(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}+2\tr\left((X-X^{*})^{\top}A^{\top}S^{\top}S\widetilde{B}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1-\epsilon)\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}-2\|X-X^{*}\|_{F}\|A^{\top}S^{\top}S\widetilde{B}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1-\epsilon)\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}-2(\epsilon/\sqrt{k})\cdot\|X-X^{*}\|_{F}\|A\|_{F}\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1-\epsilon)\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}-2\epsilon\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}.

The first inequality follows since
S
is a
(1+\epsilon)
subspace embedding of
A
, and
\tr(C^{\top}D)\geq-\|C\|_{F}\|D\|_{F}
. The second inequality follows since
S
satisfies
(\epsilon/\sqrt{k})
-Frobenius norm approximate matrix product for
A
. The last inequality follows using that
\|A\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{k}
since
A
only has
k
orthonormal columns.

Therefore,

\displaystyle(1-\epsilon)\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}-2\epsilon\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}\leq\|S(AX-B)\|_{F}^{2}-\|S\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2},

(29)

and

\displaystyle(1+\epsilon)\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}+2\epsilon\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}\geq\|S(AX-B)\|_{F}^{2}-\|S\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}.

(30)

Notice that
\widetilde{B}=AX^{*}-B=AA^{\dagger}B-B=(AA^{\dagger}-I)B,
so according to the Pythagorean theorem, we have

\displaystyle\|AX-B\|_{F}^{2}=\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}+\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2},

which means that

\displaystyle\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}=\|AX-B\|_{F}^{2}-\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}.

(31)

Using Equation (
31
), we can rewrite and lower bound the
\mathrm{LHS}
of Equation (
29
),

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1-\epsilon)\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}-2\epsilon\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}-\epsilon\left(\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}+2\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|AX-B\|_{F}^{2}-\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}-\epsilon\left(\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}+2\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|AX-B\|_{F}^{2}-\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}-\epsilon\left(\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}+\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|AX-B\|_{F}^{2}-\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}-2\epsilon\left(\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}+\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1-2\epsilon)\|AX-B\|_{F}^{2}-\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}.

(32)

The second step follows by Equation (
31
). The first inequality follows using
a^{2}+2ab<(a+b)^{2}
. The second inequality follows using
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2(a^{2}+b^{2}).
The last equality follows using
\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}+\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}=\|AX-B\|_{F}^{2}
.
Similarly, using Equation (
31
), we can rewrite and upper bound the
\mathrm{LHS}
of Equation (
30
)

\displaystyle(1+\epsilon)\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}+2\epsilon\|A(X-X^{*})\|_{F}\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}\leq(1+2\epsilon)\|AX-B\|_{F}^{2}-\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}.

(33)

Combining Equations (
29
),(
C.11.2
),(
30
),(
33
), we conclude that

\displaystyle(1-2\epsilon)\|AX-B\|_{F}^{2}-\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}\leq\|S(AX-B)\|_{F}^{2}-\|S\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+2\epsilon)\|AX-B\|_{F}^{2}-\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}.

∎

Theorem C.51
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
,
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, and
\frac{1}{2}>\epsilon>0
. Let
X^{*}
be the optimal solution to
\min_{X}\|AX-B\|_{F}^{2},
and
\widetilde{B}\equiv AX^{*}-B
. Let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
denote a sketching matrix which satisfies the following:

1.

\|S\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}\leq 100\cdot\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2},

2.

for all
X\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d},

\displaystyle(1-\epsilon)\|AX-B\|_{F}^{2}\leq\|S(AX-B)\|_{F}^{2}+\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}-\|S\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon)\|AX-B\|_{F}^{2}.

Then, for all
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
satisfying

\displaystyle\|SAX_{1}-SB\|_{F}^{2}\leq\left(1+\frac{\epsilon}{100}\right)\cdot\|SAX_{2}-SB\|_{F}^{2},

we have

\displaystyle\|AX_{1}-B\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+5\epsilon)\cdot\|AX_{2}-B\|_{F}^{2}.

Proof.

Let
A,B,S,\epsilon
be the same as in the statement of the theorem, and suppose
S
satisfies those two conditions. Let
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
satisfy

\displaystyle\|SAX_{1}-SB\|_{F}^{2}\leq\left(1+\frac{\epsilon}{100}\right)\|SAX_{2}-SB\|_{F}^{2}.

We have

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|AX_{1}-B\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\left(\|S(AX_{1}-B)\|_{F}^{2}+\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}-\|S\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\left(\left(1+\frac{\epsilon}{100}\right)\cdot\|S(AX_{2}-B)\|_{F}^{2}+\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}-\|S\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\left(\left(1+\frac{\epsilon}{100}\right)\cdot\left(\|S(AX_{2}-B)\|_{F}^{2}+\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}-\|S\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}\right)-\frac{\epsilon}{100}\cdot\left(\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}-\|S\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\cdot\left(1+\frac{\epsilon}{100}\right)\cdot\|AX_{2}-B\|_{F}^{2}-\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{100}\cdot\left(\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}-\|S\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1+3\epsilon)\|AX_{2}-B\|_{F}^{2}+\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{100}\|S\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1+3\epsilon)\|AX_{2}-B\|_{F}^{2}+2\epsilon\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1+5\epsilon)\|AX_{2}-B\|_{F}^{2}.

The first inequality follows since
S
satisfies the second condition. The second inequality follows by the relationship between
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
. The third inequality follows since
S
satisfies the second condition. The fifth inequality follows using that
\epsilon<\frac{1}{2}
and that
S
satisfies the first condition. The last inequality follows using that
\|\widetilde{B}\|_{F}^{2}=\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}^{2}\leq\|AX_{2}-B\|_{F}^{2}.

∎

Theorem C.52
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
,
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, and
\frac{1}{2}>\epsilon>0
. Let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
denote a sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix according to the leverage scores of
A
. If
S
has at least
m=O(k\log(k)/\epsilon^{2})
nonzero entries, then with probability at least
0.98
, for all
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
satisfying

\displaystyle\|SAX_{1}-SB\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\frac{\epsilon}{500})\cdot\|SAX_{2}-SB\|_{F}^{2},

we have

\displaystyle\|AX_{1}-B\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\epsilon)\cdot\|AX_{2}-B\|_{F}^{2}.

Proof.

The proof directly follows by Claim
C.49
, Theorem
C.50
and Theorem
C.51
. Because of Claim
C.49
,
S
satisfies the first condition in the statement of Theorem
C.51
with probability at least
0.99
. According to Theorem
C.50
,
S
satisfies the second condition in the statement of Theorem
C.51
with probability at least
0.99
. Thus, with probability
0.98
, by Theorem
C.51
, we complete the proof.
∎

Appendix D
Entry-wise
\ell_{1}
Norm for Arbitrary Tensors

In this section, we provide several different algorithms for tensor
\ell_{1}
-low rank approximation. Section
D.1
provides some useful facts and definitions. Section
D.2
presents several existence results. Section
D.3
describes a tool that is able to reduce the size of the objective function from
\poly(n)
to
\poly(k)
. Section
D.4
discusses the case when the problem size is small. Section
D.5
provides several bicriteria algorithms. Section
D.6
summarizes a batch of algorithms. Section
D.7
provides an algorithm for
\ell_{1}
norm CURT decomposition.

Notice that if the
\rank-k
solution does not exist, then every bicriteria algorithm in Section
D.5
can be stated in a form similar to Theorem
1.1
, and every algorithm which can output a
\rank-k
solution in Section
D.6
can be stated in a form similar to Theorem
1.2
. See Section
1
for more details.

D.1
Facts

We present a method that is able to reduce the entry-wise
\ell_{1}
-norm objective function to the Frobenius norm objective function.

Fact D.1
.

Given a
3
rd order tensor
C\in\mathbb{R}^{c_{1}\times c_{2}\times c_{3}}
, three matrices
V_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{c_{1}\times b_{1}}
,
V_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{c_{2}\times b_{2}}
,
V_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{c_{3}\times b_{3}}
, for any
k\in[1,\min_{i}b_{i}]
, if
X^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{1}\times k},X^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{2}\times k},X^{\prime}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{3}\times k}
satisfies that,

\displaystyle\|(V_{1}X^{\prime}_{1})\otimes(V_{2}X^{\prime}_{2})\otimes(V_{3}X^{\prime}_{3})-C\|_{F}\leq\alpha\min_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}}\|(V_{1}X_{1})\otimes(V_{2}X_{2})\otimes(V_{3}X_{3})-C\|_{F},

then

\displaystyle\|(V_{1}X^{\prime}_{1})\otimes(V_{2}X^{\prime}_{2})\otimes(V_{3}X^{\prime}_{3})-C\|_{1}\leq\alpha\sqrt{c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}}\min_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}}\|(V_{1}X_{1})\otimes(V_{2}X_{2})\otimes(V_{3}X_{3})-C\|_{1}.

We extend Lemma C.15 in
[
SWZ17
]
to tensors:

Fact D.2
.

Given tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}
, let
\OPT=\underset{\rank-k\penalty\ A_{k}}{\min}\|A-A_{k}\|_{1}
. For any
r\geq k
, if
\rank
-
r
tensor
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}
is an
f
-approximation to
A
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|B-A\|_{1}\leq f\cdot\OPT,

and
U,V,W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
is a
g
-approximation to
B
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|U\otimes V\otimes W-B\|_{1}\leq g\cdot\underset{\rank-k\penalty\ B_{k}}{\min}\|B_{k}-B\|_{1},

then,

\displaystyle\|U\otimes V\otimes W-A\|_{1}\lesssim gf\cdot\OPT.

Proof.

We define
\widetilde{U},\widetilde{V},\widetilde{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
to be three matrices, such that

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{U}\otimes\widetilde{V}\otimes\widetilde{W}-B\|_{1}\leq g\underset{\rank-k\penalty\ B_{k}}{\min}\|B_{k}-B\|_{1},

and also define,

\displaystyle\widehat{U},\widehat{V},\widehat{W}=\underset{U,V,W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\arg\min}\|U\otimes V\otimes W-B\|_{1}\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }U^{*},V^{*},W^{*}=\underset{U,V,W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\arg\min}\|U\otimes V\otimes W-A\|_{1}.

It is obvious that,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{U}\otimes\widehat{V}\otimes\widehat{W}-B\|_{1}\leq\|U^{*}\otimes V^{*}\otimes W^{*}-B\|_{1}.

(34)

Then,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|\widetilde{U}\otimes\widetilde{V}\otimes\widetilde{W}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|\widetilde{U}\otimes\widetilde{V}\otimes\widetilde{W}-B\|_{1}+\|B-A\|_{1}

by the triangle inequality

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ g\|\widehat{U}\otimes\widehat{V}\otimes\widehat{W}-B\|_{1}+\|B-A\|_{1}

by definition

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ g\|U^{*}\otimes V^{*}\otimes W^{*}-B\|_{1}+\|B-A\|_{1}

by Equation (
34
)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ g\|U^{*}\otimes V^{*}\otimes W^{*}-A\|_{1}+g\|B-A\|_{1}+\|B-A\|_{1}

by the triangle inequality

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ g\OPT+(g+1)\|B-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ definition\penalty\ of\penalty\ }\OPT

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ g\OPT+(g+1)f\cdot\OPT

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ since\penalty\ $B$\penalty\ is\penalty\ an\penalty\ $f$-approximation\penalty\ to\penalty\ }A

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\penalty\ gf\OPT.

This completes the proof.
∎

Using the above fact, we are able to optimize our approximation ratio.

D.2
Existence results

Definition D.3

(
\ell_{1}
multiple regression cost preserving sketch - Definition D.5 in
[
SWZ17
]
)
.

Given matrices
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r},A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
. If
\forall\beta\geq 1,\widehat{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times d}
which satisfy

\displaystyle\|SU\widehat{V}-SA\|_{1}\leq\beta\cdot\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times d}}\|SUV-SA\|_{1},

it holds that

\displaystyle\|U\widehat{V}-A\|_{1}\leq\beta\cdot c\cdot\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times d}}\|UV-A\|_{1},

then
S
provides a
c
-
\ell_{1}
-multiple-regression-cost-preserving-sketch for
(U,A)
.

Theorem D.4
.

Given a 3rd order tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}
, for any
k\geq 1
,
there exist three matrices
S_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}\times s_{1}}
,
S_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}\times s_{2}}
,
S_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}\times s_{3}}
such that

\displaystyle\min_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}(A_{1}S_{1}X_{1})_{i}\otimes(A_{2}S_{2}X_{2})_{i}\otimes(A_{3}S_{3}X_{3})_{i}-A\right\|_{1}\leq\alpha\underset{\rank-k\penalty\ A_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}}{\min}\|A_{k}-A\|_{1},

holds with probability
99/100
.

\mathrm{(I)}
. Using a dense Cauchy transform,

s_{1}=s_{2}=s_{3}=\widetilde{O}(k)
,
\alpha=\widetilde{O}(k^{1.5})\log^{3}n
.

\mathrm{(II)}
. Using a sparse Cauchy transform,

s_{1}=s_{2}=s_{3}=\widetilde{O}(k^{5})
,
\alpha=\widetilde{O}(k^{13.5})\log^{3}n
.

\mathrm{(III)}
. Guessing Lewis weights,

s_{1}=s_{2}=s_{3}=\widetilde{O}(k)
,
\alpha=\widetilde{O}(k^{1.5})
.

Proof.

We use
\OPT
to denote

\displaystyle\OPT:=\underset{\rank-k\penalty\ A_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}}{\min}\|A_{k}-A\|_{1}.

Given a tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}\times n_{3}}
, we define three matrices
A_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}n_{3}},A_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}\times n_{3}n_{1}},A_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{3}\times n_{1}n_{2}}
such that, for any
i\in[n_{1}],j\in[n_{2}],l\in[n_{3}]
,

\displaystyle A_{i,j,l}=(A_{1})_{i,(j-1)\cdot n_{3}+l}=(A_{2})_{j,(l-1)\cdot n_{1}+i}=({A}_{3})_{l,(i-1)\cdot n_{2}+j}.

We fix
V^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
and
W^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, and use
V_{1}^{*},V_{2}^{*},\cdots,V_{k}^{*}
to denote the columns of
V^{*}
and
W_{1}^{*},W_{2}^{*},\cdots,W_{k}^{*}
to denote the columns of
W^{*}
.

We consider the following optimization problem,

\displaystyle\min_{U_{1},\cdots,U_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}U_{i}\otimes V_{i}^{*}\otimes W_{i}^{*}-A\right\|_{1},

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\min_{U_{1},\cdots,U_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left\|\begin{bmatrix}U_{1}&U_{2}&\cdots&U_{k}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}V_{1}^{*}\otimes W_{1}^{*}\\
V_{2}^{*}\otimes W_{2}^{*}\\
\cdots\\
V_{k}^{*}\otimes W_{k}^{*}\end{bmatrix}-A\right\|_{1}.

We use matrix
Z_{1}
to denote
V^{*\top}\odot W^{*\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n^{2}}
and matrix
U
to denote
\begin{bmatrix}U_{1}&U_{2}&\cdots&U_{k}\end{bmatrix}
. Then we can obtain the following equivalent objective function,

\displaystyle\min_{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}\|UZ_{1}-A_{1}\|_{1}.

Choose an
\ell_{1}
multiple regression cost preserving sketch
S_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}\times s_{1}}
for
(Z_{1}^{\top},A_{1}^{\top})
. We can obtain the optimization problem,

\displaystyle\min_{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}\|UZ_{1}S_{1}-A_{1}S_{1}\|_{1}=\min_{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|U^{i}Z_{1}S_{1}-(A_{1}S_{1})^{i}\|_{1},

where
U^{i}
denotes the
i
-th row of matrix
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
and
(A_{1}S_{1})^{i}
denotes the
i
-th row of matrix
A_{1}S_{1}
. Instead of solving it under the
\ell_{1}
-norm, we consider the
\ell_{2}
-norm relaxation,

\displaystyle\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\min}\|UZ_{1}S_{1}-A_{1}S_{1}\|_{F}^{2}=\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\min}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|U^{i}Z_{1}S_{1}-(A_{1}S_{1})^{i}\|_{2}^{2}.

Let
\widehat{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
denote the optimal solution of the above optimization problem. Then,
\widehat{U}=A_{1}S_{1}(Z_{1}S_{1})^{\dagger}
. We plug
\widehat{U}
into the objective function under the
\ell_{1}
-norm. According to Claim
B.13
, we have,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{U}Z_{1}S_{1}-A_{1}S_{1}\|_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\widehat{U}^{i}Z_{1}S_{1}-(A_{1}S_{1})^{i}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{s_{1}}\min_{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}\|UZ_{1}S_{1}-A_{1}S_{1}\|_{1}.

Since
S_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}\times s_{1}}
satisfies Definition
D.3
, we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{U}Z_{1}-A_{1}\|_{1}\leq\alpha\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\min}\|UZ_{1}-A_{1}\|_{1}=\alpha\OPT,

where
\alpha=\sqrt{s_{1}}\beta
and
\beta
(see Definition
D.3
) is a parameter which depends on which kind of sketching matrix we actually choose.
It implies

\displaystyle\|\widehat{U}\otimes V^{*}\otimes W^{*}-A\|_{1}\leq\alpha\OPT.

As a second step, we fix
\widehat{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
and
W^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, and convert tensor
A
into matrix
A_{2}
. Let matrix
Z_{2}
denote
\widehat{U}^{\top}\odot W^{*\top}
. We consider the following objective function,

\displaystyle\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}\|VZ_{2}-A_{2}\|_{1},

and the optimal cost of it is at most
\alpha\OPT
.

Choose an
\ell_{1}
multiple regression cost preserving sketch
S_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}\times s_{2}}
for
(Z_{2}^{\top},A_{2}^{\top})
, and sketch on the right of the objective function to obtain this new objective function,

\displaystyle\underset{V\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\min}\|VZ_{2}S_{2}-A_{2}S_{2}\|_{1}=\min_{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|V^{i}Z_{2}S_{2}-(A_{2}S_{2})^{i}\|_{1},

where
V^{i}
denotes the
i
-th row of matrix
V
and
(A_{2}S_{2})^{i}
denotes the
i
-th row of matrix
A_{2}S_{2}
. Instead of solving this under the
\ell_{1}
-norm, we consider the
\ell_{2}
-norm relaxation,

\displaystyle\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\min}\|VZ_{2}S_{2}-A_{2}S_{2}\|_{F}^{2}=\underset{V\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\min}\|V^{i}(Z_{2}S_{2})-(A_{2}S_{2})^{i}\|_{2}^{2}.

Let
\widehat{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
denote the optimal solution of the above problem. Then
\widehat{V}=A_{2}S_{2}(Z_{2}S_{2})^{\dagger}
. By properties of the sketching matrix
S_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}\times s_{2}}
, we have,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{V}Z_{2}-A_{2}\|_{1}\leq\alpha\underset{V\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\min}\|VZ_{2}-A_{2}\|_{1}\leq\alpha^{2}\OPT,

which implies

\displaystyle\|\widehat{U}\otimes\widehat{V}\otimes W^{*}-A\|_{1}\leq\alpha^{2}\OPT.

As a third step, we fix the matrices
\widehat{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
and
\widehat{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
. We can convert tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}
into matrix
A_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}\times n}
. Let matrix
Z_{3}
denote
\widehat{U}^{\top}\odot\widehat{V}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n^{2}}
. We consider the following objective function,

\displaystyle\underset{W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\min}\|WZ_{3}-A_{3}\|_{1},

and the optimal cost of it is at most
\alpha^{2}\OPT
.

Choose an
\ell_{1}
multiple regression cost preserving sketch
S_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}\times s_{3}}
for
(Z_{3}^{\top},A_{3}^{\top})
and sketch on the right of the objective function to obtain the new objective function,

\displaystyle\underset{W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\min}\|WZ_{3}S_{3}-A_{3}S_{3}\|_{1}.

Let
\widehat{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
denote the optimal solution of the above problem. Then
\widehat{W}=A_{3}S_{3}(Z_{3}S_{3})^{\dagger}
. By properties of sketching matrix
S_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}\times s_{3}}
, we have,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{W}Z_{3}-A_{3}\|_{1}\leq\alpha\underset{W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\min}\|WZ_{3}-A_{3}\|_{1}\leq\alpha^{3}\OPT.

Thus, we obtain,

\displaystyle\min_{X_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{1}\times k},X_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{2}\times k},X_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{3}\times k}}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}(A_{1}S_{1}X_{1})_{i}\otimes(A_{2}S_{2}X_{2})_{i}\otimes(A_{3}S_{3}X_{3})_{i}-A\right\|_{1}\leq\alpha^{3}\OPT.

Proof of (I)

By Theorem C.1 in
[
SWZ17
]
, we can use dense Cauchy transforms for
S_{1},S_{2},S_{3}
, and then
s_{1}=s_{2}=s_{3}=O(k\log k)
and
\alpha=O(\sqrt{k\log k}\log n)
.

Proof of (II)

By Theorem C.1 in
[
SWZ17
]
, we can use sparse Cauchy transforms for
S_{1},S_{2},S_{3}
, and then
s_{1}=s_{2}=s_{3}=O(k^{5}\log^{5}k)
and
\alpha=O(k^{4.5}\log^{4.5}k\log n)
.

Proof of (III)

By Theorem C.1 in
[
SWZ17
]
, we can sample by Lewis weights. Then
S_{1},S_{2},S_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}\times n^{2}}
are diagonal matrices, and each of them has
O(k\log k)
nonzero rows. This gives
\alpha=O(\sqrt{k\log k})
.

∎

D.3
Polynomial in
k
size reduction

Definition D.5

(Definition D.1 in
[
SWZ17
]
)
.

Given a matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},
if matrix
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|SM\|_{1}\leq\beta\|M\|_{1},

then
S
has at most
\beta
dilation on
M
.

Definition D.6

(Definition D.2 in
[
SWZ17
]
)
.

Given a matrix
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},
if matrix
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
satisfies

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\|SUx\|_{1}\geq\frac{1}{\beta}\|Ux\|_{1},

then
S
has at most
\beta
contraction on
U
.

Theorem D.7
.

Given a tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}\times n_{3}}
and three matrices
V_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times b_{1}},V_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}\times b_{2}},V_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{3}\times b_{3}},
let
X_{1}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{1}\times k},X_{2}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{2}\times k},X_{3}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{3}\times k}
satisfies

\displaystyle X_{1}^{*},X_{2}^{*},X_{3}^{*}=\underset{X_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{1}\times k},X_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{2}\times k},X_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{3}\times k}}{\arg\min}\|V_{1}X_{1}\otimes V_{2}X_{2}\otimes V_{3}X_{3}-A\|_{1}.

Let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
have at most
\beta_{1}\geq 1
dilation on
V_{1}X_{1}^{*}\cdot((V_{2}X_{2}^{*})^{\top}\odot(V_{3}X_{3}^{*})^{\top})-A_{1}
and
S
have at most
\beta_{2}\geq 1
contraction on
V_{1}
. If
\widehat{X}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{1}\times k},\widehat{X}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{2}\times k},\widehat{X}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{3}\times k}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|SV_{1}\widehat{X}_{1}\otimes V_{2}\widehat{X}_{2}\otimes V_{3}\widehat{X}_{3}-SA\|_{1}\leq\beta\underset{X_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{1}\times k},X_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{2}\times k},X_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{3}\times k}}{\min}\|SV_{1}X_{1}\otimes V_{2}X_{2}\otimes V_{3}X_{3}-SA\|_{1},

where
\beta\geq 1
,
then

\displaystyle\|V_{1}\widehat{X}_{1}\otimes V_{2}\widehat{X}_{2}\otimes V_{3}\widehat{X}_{3}-A\|_{1}\lesssim\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\beta\min_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}}\|V_{1}X_{1}\otimes V_{2}X_{2}\otimes V_{3}X_{3}-A\|_{1}.

The proof idea is similar to
[
SWZ17
]
.

Proof.

Let
A,V_{1},V_{2},V_{3},S,X_{1}^{*},X_{2}^{*},X_{3}^{*},\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
be the same as stated in the theorem. Let

\widehat{X}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{1}\times k},\widehat{X}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{2}\times k},\widehat{X}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{3}\times k}
satisfy

\displaystyle\|SV_{1}\widehat{X}_{1}\otimes V_{2}\widehat{X}_{2}\otimes V_{3}\widehat{X}_{3}-SA\|_{1}\leq\beta\underset{X_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{1}\times k},X_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{2}\times k},X_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{3}\times k}}{\min}\|SV_{1}X_{1}\otimes V_{2}X_{2}\otimes V_{3}X_{3}-SA\|_{1}.

We have,

\displaystyle\|SV_{1}\widehat{X}_{1}\otimes V_{2}\widehat{X}_{2}\otimes V_{3}\widehat{X}_{3}-SA\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle\|SV_{1}\widehat{X}_{1}\otimes V_{2}\widehat{X}_{2}\otimes V_{3}\widehat{X}_{3}-SV_{1}X^{*}_{1}\otimes V_{2}X^{*}_{2}\otimes V_{3}X^{*}_{3}\|_{1}-\|SV_{1}X^{*}_{1}\otimes V_{2}X^{*}_{2}\otimes V_{3}X^{*}_{3}-SA\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta_{2}}\|V_{1}\widehat{X}_{1}\otimes V_{2}\widehat{X}_{2}\otimes V_{3}\widehat{X}_{3}-V_{1}X^{*}_{1}\otimes V_{2}X^{*}_{2}\otimes V_{3}X^{*}_{3}\|_{1}-\beta_{1}\|V_{1}X^{*}_{1}\otimes V_{2}X^{*}_{2}\otimes V_{3}X^{*}_{3}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta_{2}}\|V_{1}\widehat{X}_{1}\otimes V_{2}\widehat{X}_{2}\otimes V_{3}\widehat{X}_{3}-A\|_{1}-\frac{1}{\beta_{2}}\|V_{1}X^{*}_{1}\otimes V_{2}X^{*}_{2}\otimes V_{3}X^{*}_{3}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle-\beta_{1}\|V_{1}X^{*}_{1}\otimes V_{2}X^{*}_{2}\otimes V_{3}X^{*}_{3}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta_{2}}\|V_{1}\widehat{X}_{1}\otimes V_{2}\widehat{X}_{2}\otimes V_{3}\widehat{X}_{3}-A\|_{1}-(\frac{1}{\beta_{2}}+\beta_{1})\|V_{1}X^{*}_{1}\otimes V_{2}X^{*}_{2}\otimes V_{3}X^{*}_{3}-A\|_{1}.

(35)

The first and the third inequality follow by the triangle inequalities. The second inequality follows using that

\displaystyle\|SV_{1}\widehat{X}_{1}\otimes V_{2}\widehat{X}_{2}\otimes V_{3}\widehat{X}_{3}-SV_{1}X^{*}_{1}\otimes V_{2}X^{*}_{2}\otimes V_{3}X^{*}_{3}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\left\|SV_{1}(\widehat{X}_{1}-X^{*}_{1})\cdot\left((V_{2}(\widehat{X}_{2}-X^{*}_{2}))^{\top}\odot(V_{3}(\widehat{X}_{3}-X^{*}_{3}))^{\top}\right)\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta_{2}}\left\|V_{1}(\widehat{X}_{1}-X^{*}_{1})\cdot\left((V_{2}(\widehat{X}_{2}-X^{*}_{2}))^{\top}\odot(V_{3}(\widehat{X}_{3}-X^{*}_{3}))^{\top}\right)\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta_{2}}\|V_{1}\widehat{X}_{1}\otimes V_{2}\widehat{X}_{2}\otimes V_{3}\widehat{X}_{3}-V_{1}X^{*}_{1}\otimes V_{2}X^{*}_{2}\otimes V_{3}X^{*}_{3}\|_{1},

and

\displaystyle\|SV_{1}X^{*}_{1}\otimes V_{2}X^{*}_{2}\otimes V_{3}X^{*}_{3}-SA\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\|S(V_{1}X_{1}^{*}\cdot((V_{2}X_{2}^{*})^{\top}\odot(V_{3}X_{3}^{*})^{\top})-A_{1})\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\|V_{1}X_{1}^{*}\cdot((V_{2}X_{2}^{*})^{\top}\odot(V_{3}X_{3}^{*})^{\top})-A_{1}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\|V_{1}X^{*}_{1}\otimes V_{2}X^{*}_{2}\otimes V_{3}X^{*}_{3}-A\|_{1}.

(36)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\|V_{1}\widehat{X}_{1}\otimes V_{2}\widehat{X}_{2}\otimes V_{3}\widehat{X}_{3}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\beta_{2}\|SV_{1}\widehat{X}_{1}\otimes V_{2}\widehat{X}_{2}\otimes V_{3}\widehat{X}_{3}-SA\|_{1}+(1+\beta_{1}\beta_{2})\|V_{1}X^{*}_{1}\otimes V_{2}X^{*}_{2}\otimes V_{3}X^{*}_{3}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\beta_{2}\beta\|SV_{1}X^{*}_{1}\otimes V_{2}X^{*}_{2}\otimes V_{3}X^{*}_{3}-SA\|_{1}+(1+\beta_{1}\beta_{2})\|V_{1}X^{*}_{1}\otimes V_{2}X^{*}_{2}\otimes V_{3}X^{*}_{3}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\beta\|V_{1}X^{*}_{1}\otimes V_{2}X^{*}_{2}\otimes V_{3}X^{*}_{3}-A\|_{1}+(1+\beta_{1}\beta_{2})\|V_{1}X^{*}_{1}\otimes V_{2}X^{*}_{2}\otimes V_{3}X^{*}_{3}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\beta(1+2\beta_{1}\beta_{2})\|V_{1}X^{*}_{1}\otimes V_{2}X^{*}_{2}\otimes V_{3}X^{*}_{3}-A\|_{1}.

The first inequality follows by Equation (
D.3
). The second inequality follows by

\displaystyle\|SV_{1}\widehat{X}_{1}\otimes V_{2}\widehat{X}_{2}\otimes V_{3}\widehat{X}_{3}-SA\|_{1}\leq\beta\underset{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}}{\min}\|SV_{1}X_{1}\otimes V_{2}X_{2}\otimes V_{3}X_{3}-SA\|_{1}.

The third inequality follows by Equation (
36
). The final inequality follows using that
\beta\geq 1
.
∎

Algorithm 21

Reducing the Size of the Objective Function to
\poly(k)

1:

procedure

L1PolyKSizeReduction
(
A,V_{1},V_{2},V_{3},n,b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},k
)
\triangleright
Lemma
D.8

2:

for

i=1\to 3

do

3:

c_{i}\leftarrow\widetilde{O}(b_{i})
.

4:

Choose sampling and rescaling matrices
T_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{c_{i}\times n}
according to the Lewis weights of
V_{i}
.

5:

\widehat{V}_{i}\leftarrow T_{i}V_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{c_{i}\times b_{i}}
.

6:

end

for

7:

C\leftarrow A(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{c_{1}\times c_{2}\times c_{3}}
.

8:

return

\widehat{V}_{1}
,
\widehat{V}_{2}
,
\widehat{V}_{3}
and
C
.

9:

end

procedure

Lemma D.8
.

Let
\min(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3})\geq k
. Given three matrices
V_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times b_{1}}
,
V_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times b_{2}}
, and
V_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times b_{3}}
, there exists an algorithm that takes
O(\nnz(A))+n\poly(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3})
time and outputs a tensor
C\in\mathbb{R}^{c_{1}\times c_{2}\times c_{3}}
and three matrices
\widehat{V}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{c_{1}\times b_{1}}
,
\widehat{V}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{c_{2}\times b_{2}}
and
\widehat{V}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{c_{3}\times b_{3}}
with
c_{1}=c_{2}=c_{3}=\poly(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3})
, such that with probability
0.99
, for any
\alpha\geq 1
, if
X^{\prime}_{1},X^{\prime}_{2},X^{\prime}_{3}
satisfy that,

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\widehat{V}_{1}X_{1}^{\prime})_{i}\otimes(\widehat{V}_{2}X_{2}^{\prime})_{i}\otimes(\widehat{V}_{3}X_{3}^{\prime})_{i}-C\right\|_{1}\leq\alpha\underset{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}}{\min}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\widehat{V}_{1}X_{1})_{i}\otimes(\widehat{V}_{2}X_{2})_{i}\otimes(\widehat{V}_{3}X_{3})_{i}-C\right\|_{1},

then,

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}(V_{1}X_{1}^{\prime})_{i}\otimes(V_{2}X_{2}^{\prime})_{i}\otimes(V_{3}X_{3}^{\prime})_{i}-A\right\|_{1}\lesssim\alpha\min_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}({V}_{1}X_{1})_{i}\otimes({V}_{2}X_{2})_{i}\otimes({V}_{3}X_{3})_{i}-A\right\|_{1}.

Proof.

For simplicity, we define
\OPT
to be

\displaystyle\min_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}({V}_{1}X_{1})_{i}\otimes({V}_{2}X_{2})_{i}\otimes({V}_{3}X_{3})_{i}-A\right\|_{1}.

Let
T_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{c_{1}\times n}
sample according to the Lewis weights of
V_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times b_{1}}
, where
c_{1}=\widetilde{O}(b_{1})
. Let
T_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{c_{2}\times n}
sample according to the Lewis weights of
V_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times b_{2}}
, where
c_{2}=\widetilde{O}(b_{2})
. Let
T_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{c_{3}\times n}
sample according to the Lewis weights of
V_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times b_{3}}
, where
c_{3}=\widetilde{O}(b_{3})
.

For any
\alpha\geq 1,
let
X^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{1}\times k},X^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{2}\times k},X^{\prime}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{3}\times k}
satisfy

\displaystyle\|T_{1}V_{1}X^{\prime}_{1}\otimes T_{2}V_{2}X^{\prime}_{2}\otimes T_{3}V_{3}X^{\prime}_{3}-A(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\alpha\min_{X_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{1}\times k},X_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{2}\times k},X_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{3}\times k}}\|T_{1}V_{1}X_{1}\otimes T_{2}V_{2}X_{2}\otimes T_{3}V_{3}X_{3}-A(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})\|_{1}.

First, we regard
T_{1}
as the sketching matrix for the remainder. Then by Lemma D.11 in
[
SWZ17
]
and Theorem
D.7
, we have

\displaystyle\|V_{1}X^{\prime}_{1}\otimes T_{2}V_{2}X^{\prime}_{2}\otimes T_{3}V_{3}X^{\prime}_{3}-A(I,T_{2},T_{3})\|_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\penalty\

\displaystyle\alpha\min_{X_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{1}\times k},X_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{2}\times k},X_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{3}\times k}}\|V_{1}X_{1}\otimes T_{2}V_{2}X_{2}\otimes T_{3}V_{3}X_{3}-A(I,T_{2},T_{3})\|_{1}.

Second, we regard
T_{2}
as a sketching matrix for
V_{1}X_{1}\otimes V_{2}X_{2}\otimes T_{3}V_{3}X_{3}-A(I,I,T_{3})
. Then by Lemma D.11 in
[
SWZ17
]
and Theorem
D.7
, we have

\displaystyle\|V_{1}X^{\prime}_{1}\otimes V_{2}X^{\prime}_{2}\otimes T_{3}V_{3}X^{\prime}_{3}-A(I,I,T_{3})\|_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\penalty\

\displaystyle\alpha\min_{X_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{1}\times k},X_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{2}\times k},X_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{b_{3}\times k}}\|V_{1}X_{1}\otimes V_{2}X_{2}\otimes T_{3}V_{3}X_{3}-A(I,I,T_{3})\|_{1}.

Third, we regard
T_{3}
as a sketching matrix for
V_{1}X_{1}\otimes V_{2}X_{2}\otimes V_{3}X_{3}-A
. Then by Lemma D.11 in
[
SWZ17
]
and Theorem
D.7
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}(V_{1}X_{1}^{\prime})_{i}\otimes(V_{2}X_{2}^{\prime})_{i}\otimes(V_{3}X_{3}^{\prime})_{i}-A\right\|_{1}\lesssim\alpha\min_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}({V}_{1}X_{1})_{i}\otimes({V}_{2}X_{2})_{i}\otimes({V}_{3}X_{3})_{i}-A\right\|_{1}.

∎

Lemma D.9
.

Given tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}\times n_{3}}
, and two matrices
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times s},V\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}\times s}
with
\rank(U)=r
, let
T\in\mathbb{R}^{t\times n_{1}}
be a sampling/rescaling matrix according to the Lewis weights of
U
with
t=\widetilde{O}(r)
. Then with probability at least
0.99
, for all
X^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{3}\times s},\alpha\geq 1
which satisfy

\displaystyle\|T_{1}U\otimes V\otimes X^{\prime}-T_{1}A\|_{1}\leq\alpha\cdot\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{3}\times s}}\|T_{1}U\otimes V\otimes X-T_{1}A\|_{1},

it holds that

\displaystyle\|U\otimes V\otimes X^{\prime}-A\|_{1}\lesssim\alpha\cdot\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{3}\times s}}\|U\otimes V\otimes X-A\|_{1}.

The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma
D.8
.

Proof.

Let
X^{*}=\underset{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{3}\times s}}{\arg\min}\|U\otimes V\otimes X-A\|_{1}.
Then according to Lemma D.11 in
[
SWZ17
]
,
T
has at most constant dilation (Definition
D.5
) on
U\cdot(V^{\top}\odot(X^{*})^{\top})-A_{1}
, and has at most constant contraction (Definition
D.6
) on
U
. We first look at

\displaystyle\|TU\otimes V\otimes X^{\prime}-TA\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\|TU\cdot(V^{\top}\odot(X^{\prime})^{\top})-TA_{1}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle\|TU\cdot((V^{\top}\odot(X^{\prime})^{\top})-(V^{\top}\odot(X^{*})^{\top}))\|_{1}-\|TU\cdot(V^{\top}\odot(X^{*})^{\top})-TA_{1}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta_{2}}\|U\cdot((V^{\top}\odot(X^{\prime})^{\top})-A_{1}\|_{1}-(\frac{1}{\beta_{2}}+\beta_{1})\|U\cdot(V^{\top}\odot(X^{*})^{\top})-A_{1}\|_{1},

where
\beta_{1}\geq 1,\beta_{2}\geq 1
are two constants. Then we have:

\displaystyle\|U\otimes V\otimes X^{\prime}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\beta_{2}\|TU\cdot(V^{\top}\odot(X^{\prime})^{\top})-TA_{1}\|_{1}+(1+\beta_{1}\beta_{2})\|U\cdot(V^{\top}\odot(X^{*})^{\top})-A_{1}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\alpha\beta_{2}\|TU\cdot(V^{\top}\odot(X^{*})^{\top})-TA_{1}\|_{1}+(1+\beta_{1}\beta_{2})\|U\cdot(V^{\top}\odot(X^{*})^{\top})-A_{1}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\alpha\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\|U\cdot(V^{\top}\odot(X^{*})^{\top})-A_{1}\|_{1}+(1+\beta_{1}\beta_{2})\|U\cdot(V^{\top}\odot(X^{*})^{\top})-A_{1}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\penalty\

\displaystyle\alpha\|U\otimes V\otimes X^{*}-A\|_{1}.

∎

Corollary D.10
.

Given tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}
, and two matrices
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times s},V\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times s}
with
\rank(U)=r_{1},\rank(V)=r_{2}
, let
T_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{t_{1}\times n}
be a sampling/rescaling matrix according to the Lewis weights of
U
, and let
T_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{t_{2}\times n}
be a sampling/rescaling matrix according to the Lewis weights of
V
with
t_{1}=\widetilde{O}(r_{1}),t_{2}=\widetilde{O}(r_{2})
. Then with probability at least
0.99
, for all
X^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times s},\alpha\geq 1
which satisfy

\displaystyle\|T_{1}U\otimes T_{2}V\otimes X^{\prime}-A(T_{1},T_{2},I)\|_{1}\leq\alpha\cdot\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times s}}\|T_{1}U\otimes T_{2}V\otimes X-A(T_{1},T_{2},I)\|_{1},

it holds that

\displaystyle\|U\otimes V\otimes X^{\prime}-A\|_{1}\lesssim\alpha\cdot\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times s}}\|U\otimes V\otimes X-A\|_{1}.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
D.9
twice: if

\displaystyle\|T_{1}U\otimes T_{2}V\otimes X^{\prime}-A(T_{1},T_{2},I)\|_{1}\leq\alpha\cdot\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times s}}\|T_{1}U\otimes T_{2}V\otimes X-A(T_{1},T_{2},I)\|_{1},

then

\displaystyle\|U\otimes T_{2}V\otimes X^{\prime}-A(I,T_{2},I)\|_{1}\lesssim\alpha\cdot\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times s}}\|U\otimes T_{2}V\otimes X-A(I,T_{2},I)\|_{1}.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\|U\otimes V\otimes X^{\prime}-A\|_{1}\lesssim\alpha\cdot\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times s}}\|U\otimes V\otimes X-A\|_{1}.

∎

D.4
Solving small problems

Theorem D.11
.

Let
\max_{i}\{t_{i},d_{i}\}\leq n
. Given a
t_{1}\times t_{2}\times t_{3}
tensor
A
and three matrices: a
t_{1}\times d_{1}
matrix
T_{1}
, a
t_{2}\times d_{2}
matrix
T_{2}
, and a
t_{3}\times d_{3}
matrix
T_{3}
, if for
\delta>0
there exists a solution to

\displaystyle\min_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}(T_{1}X_{1})_{i}\otimes(T_{2}X_{2})_{i}\otimes(T_{3}X_{3})_{i}-A\right\|_{1}:=\OPT,

such that each entry of
X_{i}
can be expressed using
O(n^{\delta})
bits, then there exists an algorithm that takes
n^{O(\delta)}\cdot 2^{O(d_{1}k+d_{2}k+d_{3}k)}
time and outputs three matrices:
\widehat{X}_{1}
,
\widehat{X}_{2}
, and
\widehat{X}_{3}
such that
\|(T_{1}\widehat{X}_{1})\otimes(T_{2}\widehat{X}_{2})\otimes(T_{3}\widehat{X}_{3})-A\|_{1}=\OPT
.

Proof.

For each
i\in[3]
, we can create
t_{i}\times d_{i}
variables to represent matrix
X_{i}
. Let
x
denote the list of these variables. Let
B
denote tensor
\sum_{i=1}^{k}(T_{1}X_{1})_{i}\otimes(T_{2}X_{2})_{i}\otimes(T_{3}X_{3})_{i}
. Then we can write the following objective function,

\displaystyle\min_{x}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{t_{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{t_{3}}|B_{i,j,l}(x)-A_{i,j,l}|.

To remove the
|\cdot|
, we create
t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}
extra variables
\sigma_{i,j,l}
. Then we obtain the objective function:

\displaystyle\min_{x,\sigma}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{t_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{t_{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{t_{3}}\sigma_{i,j,l}(B_{i,j,l}(x)-A_{i,j,l})

s.t.

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sigma_{i,j,l}^{2}=1,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sigma_{i,j,l}(B_{i,j,l}(x)-A_{i,j,l})\geq 0,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|x\|_{2}^{2}+\|\sigma\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2^{O(n^{\delta})}

where the last constraint is unharmful, because there exists a solution that can be written using
O(n^{\delta})
bits. Note that the number of inequality constraints in the above system is
O(t_{1}t_{2}t_{3})
, the degree is
O(1)
, and the number of variables is
v=(t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}+d_{1}k+d_{2}k+d_{3}k)
. Thus by Theorem
B.11
, we know that the minimum nonzero cost is at least

\displaystyle(2^{O(n^{\delta})})^{-2^{\widetilde{O}(v)}}.

It is immediate that the upper bound on cost is at most
2^{O(n^{\delta})}
, and thus the number of binary search steps is at most
\log(2^{O(n^{\delta})})2^{\widetilde{O}(v)}
. In each step of the binary search, we need to choose a cost
C
between the lower bound and the upper bound, and write down the polynomial system,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{t_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{t_{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{t_{3}}\sigma_{i,j,l}(B_{i,j,l}(x)-A_{i,j,l})\leq C,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sigma_{i,j,l}^{2}=1,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sigma_{i,j,l}(B_{i,j,l}(x)-A_{i,j,l})\geq 0,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|x\|_{2}^{2}+\|\sigma\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2^{O(n^{\delta})}.

Using Theorem
B.10
, we can determine if there exists a solution to the above polynomial system. Since the number of variables is
v
, and the degree is
O(1)
, the number of inequality constraints is
t_{1}t_{2}t_{2}
. Thus, the running time is

\displaystyle\poly(\text{bitsize})\cdot(\#\constraints\cdot\degree)^{\#\variables}=n^{O(\delta)}2^{\widetilde{O}(v)}

∎

D.5
Bicriteria algorithms

We present several bicriteria algorithms with different tradeoffs. We first present an algorithm that runs in nearly linear time and outputs a solution with rank
\widetilde{O}(k^{3})
in Theorem
D.12
. Then we show an algorithm that runs in
\nnz(A)
time but outputs a solution with rank
\poly(k)
in Theorem
D.13
. Then we explain an idea which is able to decrease the cubic rank to quadratic rank, and thus we can obtain Theorem
D.14
and Theorem
D.15
.

D.5.1
Input sparsity time

Theorem D.12
.

Given a
3
rd order tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}
, for any
k\geq 1
,
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, let
r=\widetilde{O}(k^{3})
. There exists an algorithm which takes
\nnz(A)\cdot\widetilde{O}(k)+O(n)\poly(k)+\poly(k)
time and outputs three matrices
U,V,W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{r}U_{i}\otimes V_{i}\otimes W_{i}-A\right\|_{1}\leq\widetilde{O}(k^{3/2})\log^{3}n\min_{\rank-k\penalty\ A_{k}}\|A_{k}-A\|_{1}

holds with probability
9/10
.

Proof.

We first choose three dense Cauchy transforms
S_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}\times s_{i}}
. According to Section
B.7
, for each
i\in[3]
,
A_{i}S_{i}
can be computed in
\nnz(A)\cdot\widetilde{O}(k)
time. Then we apply Lemma
D.8
(Algorithm
21
). We obtain three matrices
Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3}
and a tensor
C
. Note that for each
i\in[3]
,
Y_{i}
can be computed in
n\poly(k)
time. Because
C=A(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})
and
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\widetilde{O}(k)}
are three sampling and rescaling matrices,
C
can be computed in
\nnz(A)+\widetilde{O}(k^{3})
time. At the end, we just need to run an
\ell_{1}
-regression solver to find the solution to the problem,

\displaystyle\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{1}\times s_{2}\times s_{3}}}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{s_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{s_{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{s_{3}}X_{i,j,l}(Y_{1})_{i}\otimes(Y_{2})_{j}\otimes(Y_{3})_{j}\right\|_{1},

where
(Y_{1})_{i}
denotes the
i
-th column of matrix
Y_{1}
. Since the size of the above problem is only
\poly(k)
, this can be solved in
\poly(k)
time.
∎

.

Theorem D.13
.

Given a
3
rd order tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}
, for any
k\geq 1
,
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, let
r=\widetilde{O}(k^{15})
. There exists an algorithm that takes
\nnz(A)+O(n)\poly(k)+\poly(k)
time and outputs three matrices
U,V,W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{r}U_{i}\otimes V_{i}\otimes W_{i}-A\right\|_{1}\leq\poly(k,\log n)\min_{\rank-k\penalty\ A_{k}}\|A_{k}-A\|_{1}

holds with probability
9/10
.

Proof.

We first choose three dense Cauchy transforms
S_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}\times s_{i}}
. According to Section
B.7
, for each
i\in[3]
,
A_{i}S_{i}
can be computed in
O(\nnz(A))
time. Then we apply Lemma
D.8
(Algorithm
21
), and can obtain three matrices
Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3}
and a tensor
C
. Note that for each
i\in[3]
,
Y_{i}
can be computed in
O(n)\poly(k)
time. Because
C=A(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})
and
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\widetilde{O}(k)}
are three sampling and rescaling matrices,
C
can be computed in
\nnz(A)+\widetilde{O}(k^{3})
time. At the end, we just need to run an
\ell_{1}
-regression solver to find the solution to the problem,

\displaystyle\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{1}\times s_{2}\times s_{3}}}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{s_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{s_{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{s_{3}}X_{i,j,l}(Y_{1})_{i}\otimes(Y_{2})_{j}\otimes(Y_{3})_{l}-C\right\|_{1},

where
(Y_{1})_{i}
denotes the
i
-th column of matrix
Y_{1}
. Since the size of the above problem is only
\poly(k)
, it can be solved in
\poly(k)
time.
∎

D.5.2
Improving cubic rank to quadratic rank

Algorithm 24

\ell_{1}
-Low Rank Approximation, Bicriteria Algorithm,

, Nearly Input Sparsity Time

procedure

L1BicriteriaAlgorithm
(

)
\triangleright
Theorem
D.14

s_{1}\leftarrow s_{2}\leftarrow s_{3}\leftarrow\widetilde{O}(k)
.

3:

For each
i\in[3]
, choose
S_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}\times s_{i}}
to be a dense Cauchy transform.
\triangleright
Part (I) of Theorem
D.2

4:

Compute
A_{1}\cdot S_{1}
,
A_{2}\cdot S_{2}
.

5:

For each
i\in[2]
, choose
T_{i}
to be a sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix according to the Lewis weights of
A_{i}S_{i}
, with
t_{i}=\widetilde{O}(k)
nonzero entries.

6:

C\leftarrow A(T_{1},T_{2},I)
.

7:

B^{i+(j-1)s_{1}}\leftarrow\vect((T_{1}A_{1}S_{1})_{i}\otimes(T_{2}A_{2}S_{2})_{j}),\forall i\in[s_{1}],j\in[s_{2}]
.

8:

Form objective function
\min_{W}\|WB-C_{3}\|_{1}

9:

Run
\ell_{1}
-regression solver to find
\widehat{W}
.

10:

Construct
\widehat{U}
by using
A_{1}S_{1}
according to Equation (
38
).

11:

Construct
\widehat{V}
by using
A_{2}S_{2}
according to Equation (
39
).

12:

return

\widehat{U},\widehat{V},\widehat{W}
.

13:

end

procedure

Theorem D.14
.

Given a
3
rd order tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}
, for any
k\geq 1
,
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, let
r=\widetilde{O}(k^{2})
. There exists an algorithm which takes
\nnz(A)\cdot\widetilde{O}(k)+O(n)\poly(k)+\poly(k)
time and outputs three matrices
U,V,W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{r}U_{i}\otimes V_{i}\otimes W_{i}-A\right\|_{1}\leq\widetilde{O}(k^{3/2})\log^{3}n\min_{\rank-k\penalty\ A_{k}}\|A_{k}-A\|_{1}

holds with probability
9/10
.

Proof.

Let
\OPT=\underset{A_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}}{\min}\|A_{k}-A\|_{1}.

We first choose three dense Cauchy transforms
S_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}\times s_{i}}
,
\forall i\in[3]
. According to Section
B.7
, for each
i\in[3]
,
A_{i}S_{i}
can be computed in
\nnz(A)\cdot\widetilde{O}(k)
time. Then we choose
T_{i}
to be a sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix according to the Lewis weights of
A_{i}S_{i}
,
\forall i\in[2]
.

According to Theorem
D.4
, we have

\displaystyle\min_{X_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{1}\times k},X_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{2}\times k},X_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{3}\times k}}\left\|\sum_{l=1}^{k}(A_{1}S_{1}X_{1})_{l}\otimes(A_{2}S_{2}X_{2})_{l}\otimes(A_{3}S_{3}X_{3})_{l}-A\right\|_{1}\leq\widetilde{O}(k^{1.5})\log^{3}n\OPT

Now we fix an
l
and we have:

\displaystyle(A_{1}S_{1}X_{1})_{l}\otimes(A_{2}S_{2}X_{2})_{l}\otimes(A_{3}S_{3}X_{3})_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{s_{1}}(A_{1}S_{1})_{i}(X_{1})_{i,l}\right)\otimes\left(\sum_{j=1}^{s_{2}}(A_{2}S_{2})_{j}(X_{2})_{j,l}\right)\otimes(A_{3}S_{3}X_{3})_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{s_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{s_{2}}(A_{1}S_{1})_{i}\otimes(A_{2}S_{2})_{j}\otimes(A_{3}S_{3}X_{3})_{l}(X_{1})_{i,l}(X_{2})_{j,l}

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\min_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{s_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{s_{2}}(A_{1}S_{1})_{i}\otimes(A_{2}S_{2})_{j}\otimes\left(\sum_{l=1}^{k}(A_{3}S_{3}X_{3})_{l}(X_{1})_{i,l}(X_{2})_{j,l}\right)-A\right\|_{1}\leq\widetilde{O}(k^{1.5})\log^{3}n\OPT.

(37)

We create matrix
\widehat{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times s_{1}s_{2}}
by copying matrix
A_{1}S_{1}

s_{2}
times, i.e.,

\displaystyle\widehat{U}=\begin{bmatrix}A_{1}S_{1}&A_{1}S_{1}&\cdots&A_{1}S_{1}\end{bmatrix}.

(38)

We create matrix
\widehat{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times s_{1}s_{2}}
by copying the
i
-th column of
A_{2}S_{2}
a total of
s_{1}
times into the columns
(i-1)s_{1},\cdots,is_{1}
of
\widehat{V}
, for each
i\in[s_{2}]
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\widehat{V}=\begin{bmatrix}(A_{2}S_{2})_{1}&\cdots&(A_{2}S_{2})_{1}&(A_{2}S_{2})_{2}&\cdots&(A_{2}S_{2})_{2}&\cdots(A_{2}S_{2})_{s_{2}}&\cdots&(A_{2}S_{2})_{s_{2}}.\end{bmatrix}

(39)

According to Equation (
37
), we have:

\displaystyle\underset{W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times s_{1}s_{2}}}{\min}\|\widehat{U}\otimes\widehat{V}\otimes W-A\|_{1}\leq\widetilde{O}(k^{1.5})\log^{3}n\cdot\OPT.

Let

\displaystyle\widehat{W}=\underset{W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times s_{1}s_{2}}}{\arg\min}\|T_{1}\widehat{U}\otimes T_{2}\widehat{V}\otimes W-A(T_{1},T_{2},I)\|_{1}.

Due to Corollary
D.10
, we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{U}\otimes\widehat{V}\otimes\widehat{W}-A\|_{1}\leq\widetilde{O}(k^{1.5})\log^{3}n\cdot\OPT.

Putting it all together, we have that
\widehat{U},\widehat{V},\widehat{W}
gives a rank-
\widetilde{O}(k^{2})
bicriteria algorithm to the original problem.
∎

Theorem D.15
.

Given a
3
rd order tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}
, for any
k\geq 1
,
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, let
r=\widetilde{O}(k^{10})
. There exists an algorithm which takes
\nnz(A)+O(n)\poly(k)+\poly(k)
time and outputs three matrices
U,V,W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{r}U_{i}\otimes V_{i}\otimes W_{i}-A\right\|_{1}\leq\poly(k,\log n)\min_{\rank-k\penalty\ A_{k}}\|A_{k}-A\|_{1}

holds with probability
9/10
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Theorem
D.14
. The only difference is that instead of choosing dense Cauchy matrices
S_{1},S_{2}
, we choose sparse Cauchy matrices.
∎

Notice that if we firstly apply a sparse Cauchy transform, we can reduce the rank of the matrix to
\poly(k)
. Then we apply a dense Cauchy transform and can further reduce the dimension while only incurring another
\poly(k)
factor in the approximation ratio. By combining a sparse Cauchy transform and a dense Cauchy transform, we can improve the running time from
\nnz(A)\cdot\widetilde{O}(k)
to
\nnz(A)
.

Corollary D.16
.

Given a
3
rd order tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}
, for any
k\geq 1
,
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, let
r=\widetilde{O}(k^{2})
. There exists an algorithm which takes
\nnz(A)+O(n)\poly(k)+\poly(k)
time and outputs three matrices
U,V,W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{r}U_{i}\otimes V_{i}\otimes W_{i}-A\right\|_{1}\leq\poly(k,\log n)\min_{\rank-k\penalty\ A_{k}}\|A_{k}-A\|_{1}

holds with probability
9/10
.

Algorithm 26

D.6
Algorithms

In this section, we show two different algorithms by using different kind of sketches. One is shown in Theorem
D.17
which gives a fast running time. Another one is shown in Theorem
D.19
which gives the best approximation ratio.

.

Theorem D.17
.

Given a
3
rd tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}
, for any
k\geq 1
, there exists an algorithm that takes
\nnz(A)\cdot\widetilde{O}(k)+O(n)\poly(k)+2^{\widetilde{O}(k^{2})}
time and outputs three matrices
U,V,W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
such that,

\displaystyle\left\|U\otimes V\otimes W-A\right\|_{1}\leq\poly(k,\log n)\min_{\rank-k\penalty\ A^{\prime}}\|A^{\prime}-A\|_{1}.

holds with probability at least
9/10
.

Proof.

First, we apply part (II) of Theorem
D.4
. Then
A_{i}S_{i}
can be computed in
O(\nnz(A))
time. Second, we use Lemma
D.8
to reduce the size of the objective function from
O(n^{3})
to
\poly(k)
in
n\poly(k)
time by only losing a constant factor in approximation ratio. Third, we use Claim
B.15
to relax the objective function from entry-wise
\ell_{1}
-norm to Frobenius norm, and this step causes us to lose some other
\poly(k)
factors in approximation ratio. As a last step, we use Theorem
C.45
to solve the Frobenius norm objective function.
∎

Notice again that if we first apply a sparse Cauchy transform, we can reduce the rank of the matrix to
\poly(k)
. Then as before we can apply a dense Cauchy transform to further reduce the dimension while only incurring another
\poly(k)
factor in the approximation ratio. By combining a sparse Cauchy transform and a dense Cauchy transform, we can improve the running time from
\nnz(A)\cdot\widetilde{O}(k)
to
\nnz(A)
, while losing some additional
\poly(k)
factors in approximation ratio.

Corollary D.18
.

Given a
3
rd tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}
, for any
k\geq 1
, there exists an algorithm that takes
\nnz(A)+O(n)\poly(k)+2^{\widetilde{O}(k^{2})}
time and outputs three matrices
U,V,W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
such that,

\displaystyle\left\|U\otimes V\otimes W-A\right\|_{1}\leq\poly(k,\log n)\min_{\rank-k\penalty\ A^{\prime}}\|A^{\prime}-A\|_{1}.

holds with probability at least
9/10
.

Theorem D.19
.

Given a
3
rd order tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}
, for any
k\geq 1
, there exists an algorithm that takes
n^{\widetilde{O}(k)}2^{\widetilde{O}(k^{3})}
time and output three matrices
U,V,W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
such that,

\displaystyle\|U\otimes V\otimes W-A\|_{1}\leq\widetilde{O}(k^{3/2})\min_{\rank-k\penalty\ A^{\prime}}\|A^{\prime}-A\|_{1}.

holds with probability at least
9/10
.

Proof.

First, we apply part (III) of Theorem
D.4
. Then, guessing
S_{i}
requires
n^{\widetilde{O}(k)}
time. Second, we use Lemma
D.8
to reduce the size of the objective from
O(n^{3})
to
\poly(k)
in polynomial time while only losing a constant factor in approximation ratio. Third, we use Theorem
D.11
to solve the entry-wise
\ell_{1}
-norm objective function directly.
∎

Algorithm 29

-CURT Decomposition Algorithm

1:

procedure

L1CURT
(
A,U_{B},V_{B},W_{B},n,k
)
\triangleright
Theorem
D.20

2:

Form
B_{1}=V_{B}^{\top}\odot W_{B}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n^{2}}
.

3:

Let
D_{1}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}\times n^{2}}
be the sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix corresponding to the Lewis weights of
B_{1}^{\top}
, and let
D_{1}
have
d_{1}=O(k\log k)
nonzero entries.

4:

Form
\widehat{U}=A_{1}D_{1}(B_{1}D_{1})^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
.

5:

Form
B_{2}=\widehat{U}^{\top}\odot W_{B}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n^{2}}
.

6:

Let
D_{2}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}\times n^{2}}
be the sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix corresponding to the Lewis weights of
B_{2}^{\top}
, and let
D_{2}
have
d_{2}=O(k\log k)
nonzero entries.

7:

Form
\widehat{V}=A_{2}D_{2}(B_{2}D_{2})^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
.

8:

Form
B_{3}=\widehat{U}^{\top}\odot\widehat{V}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n^{2}}
.

9:

, and let

have

nonzero entries.

,

,

.

.

12:

C
,

,

and

.
